Board of Supervisors Regular 6. b.        
Meeting Date: 05/23/2023  
Title: Wilson & Company - Starlight Professional Services Agreement-Dist IV
Submitted For: Madhav Mundle, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Jeanine Carruthers, Deputy Director Administration
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of a Professional Services contract with Wilson & Company for the Starlight
Pavement Reconstruction Final Design.
Background:
Wilson & Company will perform the engineering services required to prepare a set of
construction documents which consist of pavement rehabilitation plans of roughly 7,500 linear
feet of existing roadway. The project will also evaluate the roadway geometry for turning
movements at all the intersections within the project limits. Additional intersection pavement
widening may be required to improve off tracking of vehicles. Drainage improvements within
the project limits will be limited to the additional embankment curb to control sheet flows on
the roadway. If intersection widening requires extending of cross culvert pipes that will be
included in the final construction documents.
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval of this contract.
Fiscal Impact
Fiscal Year: FY 2023-24
Budgeted Y/N: Y
Amount Requested: 72314.67
Fiscal Impact:
Funds are budgeted in the HURF budget under Professional Services.

NOW, THEREFORE, the parties agree as follows: 
 
1. Services.  Consultant shall perform the work described in the Scope of Work dated 
April 12, 2023, and those additional items set forth in the Consultant’s schedule and 
fee proposal dated April 12, 2023, in a competent and professional manner to the

proposal are attached hereto and by this reference incorporat ed herein.  If any 
incorporated term is inconsistent with the Agreement, this Agreement shall control.

capacity and professional expertise to perfo rm this Agreement.  Consultant shall 
provide an adequate staff of experienced  personnel, capable of and devoted to the 
successful accomplishment of the work to be performed hereunder.  Consultant shall 
assign specific individuals to key positio ns.  Once assigned to work under this 
Agreement, key personnel shall not be removed or replaced without County’s prior 
written approval.  
 
3. Independent Contractor.  Consultant acknowledges that  it is an independent 
contractor.  Neither party is or shall purport to be an agent, employee, partner, joint 
venturer or associate of the other.  An employee or agen t of one party shall not be 
deemed an employer or agent  of the other party for any purpose.  Taxes or Social 
Security contributions will not be with held from any payment by County, and 
Consultant shall be solely responsible for such matters.

4. Subcontracts.  Consultant shall not enter into any subcontract with respect to any of 
the work to be performed hereunder without County’s prior written approval.  All 
subcontracts shall comply with applicable federal and state laws and regulations and 
shall impose on the subcontractor substantially the same obligations as are imposed 
on Consultant by this Agreement with respect to those matters covered by Sections 8, 
9, 10, 12, 15 and 18.  Consultant is  responsible for full performance of this

and the orderly progress of the Project, and in accord ance with an agreed upon

parties’ representatives. 
 
6. Payment.  County shall pay to Consultant  a not-to-exceed sum of seventy two

Payments shall be made within 30 days of County’s receipt of Consultant’s monthly 
invoices.  Each invoice shall detail the work performed du ring the billing period.  
Invoice amounts in percent of Project co st shall not exceed the percentage of 
completion of the Project as approved by County.

SUBJECT:  Scope and Fee Proposal – Revised Limited Final Design  
Starlight Pavement Reconstruction project 
 
Dear Mr. Mundle: 
 
Wilson & Company, Inc., Engineers & Architects (Wilson & Company) is pleased to submit this 
proposal to provide professional engineering services and consulting services to prepare a final 
design and construction documents for Phase I and Phase II of the Starlight Subdivision Pavement 
Reconstruction Project in Show Low, Arizona.  
 
Thank you very much for this opportunity to collaborate with you on this project. We are confident 
you will be pleased with the quality, timeliness, and responsiveness of our services and work 
documents. 
 
PROJECT LOCATION AND LIMITS 
Starlight Ridge subdivision is located just south of Show Low City limits in Navajo County, Arizona. 
It is located west of State Route 260 and has access from Webb Drive and Wagon Wheel Lane. 
 
The Starlight Ridge Pavement Reconstruction Project limits are shown in Figure 1. The project 
consists of pavement reconstruction plans for the following roadways within Phase I and Phase II of 
the Starlight Ridge development.  
 
The roadways and their estimated lengths that are anticipated to be included in the overall 
reconstruction project are shown in the following table. (RED-roads final construction only) 
 
Table 1 Anticipated Reconstructed Roadways 
# Street To From Length (ft) 
1 Orion Dr Rim Rd end 235 
2 Rim Rd Orion Dr Andromeda Wy 3,000 
3 Gemini Ln Rim Rd Wagon Wheel Ln 500 
4 Carina Ln Rim Rd Wagon Wheel Ln 825 
5 Taurus Ln Wagon Wheel Ln end 225 
6 Draco Dr Wagon Wheel Ln end 330 
7 Andromeda Wy Rim Rd Luminary Wy 690 
8 Luminary Wy Andromeda Way Wagon Wheel Ln 1,125 
9 Wagon Wheel Ln Taurus Dr end of project 4,025 
10 Leo Ln Wagon Wheel Ln Starlight Ridge Rd 1,050 
11 Starlight Ridge Rd Leo Ln Wagon Wheel Ln 2,425 
12 Aries Cir Starlight Ridge Rd end 300 
13 Webb Dr Starlight Ridge Rd end 190

<!-- page 166 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
pg. 2 
 
 
 
Figure 1: Project Limits 
 
SCOPE OF WORK 
Wilson & Company will perform the engineering services required to prepare a set of construction 
documents which consist of pavement rehabilitation plans of roughly 15,000 linear feet of existing 
roadway. The project will also evaluate the roadway geometry for turning movements at all the 
intersections within the project limits. Additional intersection pavement widening may be required 
to improve off tracking of vehicles. Drainage improvements within the project limits will be limited 
to the additional of embankment curb to control sheet flows on the roadway. If intersection 
widening requires extending of cross culvert pipes that will be included in the final construction 
documents. 
 
Proposed pavement improvements will tie into existing driveways at existing grades to eliminate the 
need for temporary construction easements. It is Wilson & Company’s understanding that the 
construction of these proposed improvements will be phased over a number of years as funding 
becomes available. The survey and conceptual phasing will be completed for the entire subdivision. 
The roads indicated in red in Table 1 and shown as green lines in Figure 1 will be developed to final 
construction plans.

<!-- page 167 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
pg. 3 
 
The plan set will include construction detail sheets at intersection and driveway crossings, in addition 
to, signing and pavement marking plans. While performing these services, Wilson & Company will 
use engineering judgment, practices and principles that could be reasonably expected from other 
engineers under like circumstances. 
 
Based on the information above the following scope of services, project schedule and fee proposal 
are as followed: 
 
PROJECT SCOPE OF WORK 
 
A. PROJECT MANAGEMENT/MEETINGS/COORDINATION 
a. Project set-up – Manage contract agreement, process invoicing, and prepare 
progress reports.  
b. Meetings/communication – All meetings are assumed to be virtual using 
Microsoft Teams or similar software application unless otherwise noted (on-site). 
The following meetings are anticipated during the duration of this project: 
i. Draft Concept-Level Roll-Plot review meeting – 2 staff x 1 meeting 
ii. 90% Plans, Specifications, & Estimate review meeting – 2 staff x 1 
meeting 
iii. 100% Plans, Specifications, & Estimate review meeting – 2 staff x 1 
meeting 
 
B. DATA COLLECTION & FIELD INVESTIGATION 
 
After a notice-to-proceed has been issued, Wilson & Company will request the following items 
from the COUNTY to provide: 
• GIS files, including but not limited to parcels, utilities, etc. 
 
Topographical Survey:  Wilson & Company, Inc. will complete office and field survey tasks 
related to the performance of one Aerial Topographic Survey supplemented with a ground 
survey to more fully detail and identify existing utilities, curb transitions, driveways, and other 
transition areas to commensurate proposed roadway improvements. The project area to be 
mapped is as shown in the blue shaded areas in Figure 1. The project area is located within the 
North Half of Section 8, Township 9 North, Range 22 East of the Gila & Salt River Meridian, 
City of Show Low, Navajo County, Arizona. 
 
Field control and data collection shall be subject to the following NGS Control Designation 
Points Q502 & Show Low Lake both located approx. 1 mile to the east of the project: 
 
Horizontal Control: NAD83 Arizona State Plane Coordinate System, East Zone Projection, 
International Feet  
Vertical Control: North American Vertical Datum (NAVD88) 
 
Field Control and Aerial Survey Panel Placement:  Wilson & Company will have field crews 
establish a control network utilizing the NGS control points mentioned above. The field crews 
will then establish ten (10) aerial control panels to support aerial mapping efforts to be 
performed by Cooper Aerial Surveys Company (see Cooper Aerial Surveys Company proposal 
attached).

<!-- page 168 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
pg. 4 
 
 
Wilson & Company field crews will complete a supplemental ground topographic survey to 
collect existing utilities (excluding sewer manhole inverts) within the outlined project area along 
with collecting/detailing approx. 30 driveway and/or roadway intersections. Collecting GPS 
shots at edge of driveway pavement and collecting the existing curb terminations at intersections 
will help provide a base mapping file conducive to Civil Engineering Design.  
 
Wilson & Company will collect any found roadway monuments. The field crew will establish a 
pk nail at all major roadway intersections where no existing roadway monuments exist. The set 
pk nails will be utilized on engineers plans to help establish roadway control, alignments, and 
stationing. 
 
We have assumed that we will have unrestricted access to perform the survey. 
 
Exclusions: 
Right of Way calculations and base mapping 
Legal Descriptions and Easement development 
 
Deliverables: 
Civil 3D 2021 file of Aerial Topographic Survey Map 
Civil 3D 2021 file of Geometric Control Sheet signed by an Arizona Registered Land Surveyor 
Text file of all points set and collected in P,N,E,EL,Desc format 
**See Cooper Aerial Surveys Company proposal for additional deliverables**  
 
Utilities:  Wilson & Company will request from AZ 811 the list of utility owners and their 
facilities within the project limits. Wilson & Company will be responsible for obtaining no-
conflict clearance letters from the utility owners based on the 90% design concept. The County 
will assist in coordination efforts, on an as needed basis. 
 
Environmental:  Wilson & Company will review typical environmental resource databases for 
the project area. These will include AZSITE for cultural resources, the US Fish and Wildlife 
Service's IPaC for threatened & endangered species, as well as ADEQ, NEPAssist, and the 
EPA's EnviroMapper for hazardous materials. Results of these database searches will be 
documented in a letter for the project file. This scope does not include any field visits or surveys 
or consultation with any state or federal agencies. 
 
Drainage:  Wilson & Company is under the assumption that there is no change in the existing 
drainage patterns or existing drainage features since the existing pavement limits to be 
reconstructed will be similar. If the final design limits change substantially then the need for 
further drainage on-site analysis is determined to be warranted, this additional effort will need to 
be addressed with a change order to this original scope of work. This will be negotiated with the 
County if they agree that it is required.  
 
Geotechnical Investigation and Pavement Design:  Wilson & Company will use the 
Geotechnical Report dated February 17, 2021, prepared by Speedie & Associates for the Navajo 
County Engineering Department as the basis of the recommended improvements. This

<!-- page 169 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
pg. 5 
 
document provides the results of the investigation and recommendations for evaluation of the 
existing pavement section for the Starlight Ridge Units I & II area.  
 
C. SCOPING DESIGN 
 
Wilson & Company will prepare a draft design concept for the roadway and intersection 
improvements on a roll-plot and construction cost estimates.  
 
The Concept-level Design Roll Plot exhibit (PDF) will communicate graphically the 
recommended locations for improvements, including any potential intersection geometry 
modifications, drainage enhancements, and other details. We will utilize the flown aerial imagery 
for background and supplemented topographical survey to show proposed improvements.  
 
Deliverable: Concept-level Design Roll Plot (50 scale PDF) 
Comment Resolution meeting with the County to address design. (Virtual meeting) 
 
D. FINAL DESIGN 
 
a. 90% Design Plans, Specifications & Estimate – Once the County approves of 
the Concept-level Design, Wilson & Company will prepare 90% design plans, 
specifications, and estimate. It is assumed that standard construction specifications 
will be used, and only special provisions will be required to be developed by Wilson 
& Company, if needed. The design plans will include typical sections showing 
roadway reconstruction, details of roadway intersections, typical connections to 
existing driveways, details of any drainage enhancements, (embankment curb 
locations), and sign and pavement markings. Wilson & Company will also develop a 
construction phasing and high-level traffic control plan for typical construction 
operations. A detailed construction cost estimate will be developed at this stage. 
 
Construction plans for 90%, and 100% anticipate including the following sheets: 
▪ Cover Sheet (1 sheet) 
▪ General Notes (1 sheet) 
▪ Keymap/Survey Control (2 sheets) 
▪ Typical Sections (2 sheets) 
▪ Storm Water Pollution Protection Plans – Details (1 sheet) 
▪ Miscellaneous Roadway Intersection & Driveway Details (6 sheets) 
▪ Typical Traffic Control Work Zone Details and Phasing Plans (6 sheets) 
▪ Signing and Pavement Marking plans (2 Sheets) 
 
Project Specifications will be prepared for items not covered by local construction 
standards and details acceptable to Navajo County Public Works.  
 
Cost Estimate – Project quantities will be prepared for the project and put into a bid 
schedule. Unit prices will be developed based upon available historical bid tabulations.  
 
Deliverable: 90% Design Plans, Specifications, and Estimate (11”x17” PDF) 
Comment Resolution meeting with the County to address design. (Virtual meeting)

<!-- page 170 -->
Navajo County Public Works  
Joseph City Pedestrian Improvements Project 
pg. 6 
 
 
b. 100% Final Design Plans, Specifications & Estimate – Wilson & Company will 
address the 90% comments from the County and provide final plans based on the 
agreed resolution. 
 
Deliverable: 100% Sealed Plans, Specifications, and Estimate (11”x17” PDF) 
Comment Resolution meeting with the County to address design. (Virtual meeting) 
 
E. QUALITY ASSURANCE / QUALITY CONTROL (QA/QC) 
Wilson & Company will follow its company QA/QC procedures which includes performing 
an internal review of the deliverable submittal. This internal process is integral for delivering 
the highest quality product and minimizing risk.  
F. SCHEDULE 
We will begin work upon execution of the contract and issuance of ‘Notice to Proceed’ to 
begin the design phase of the project. Final design schedule will be determined after the 
scoping phase of development. Anticipated schedule is attached as Exhibit A. 
 
G. SERVICE ELEMENTS NOT INCLUDED 
The following services are not included in this proposal. If such services are required, a contract 
modification can be executed to Wilson & Company to provide these services: 
▪ Environmental Services that include any field visits or surveys or consultation with 
any state or federal agencies 
▪ Geotechnical Engineering services or materials testing (assume use report that was 
recently conducted) 
▪ Right-of-Way Legal Descriptions/Exhibit (no new right-of-way) 
▪ Utility Designation or Location Survey 
▪ Landscape Architecture/Irrigation 
▪ Off-Site Drainage analysis or design 
▪ Traffic Count/Traffic Signal/Traffic Analysis  
▪ Public Information Meetings 
▪ Permit Fees 
▪ City/County Review Fees 
▪ Streetlighting Design

includes subconsultants, project related expenses, and delivery costs. The project will bill 
monthly by percent complete.

Board of Supervisors Regular 6. c.        
Meeting Date: 05/23/2023  
Title: Task Agreement No. 31- N8065 Gravel
Submitted For: Madhav Mundle, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Miles Begay, Tribal Government Relations Liaison
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of Task Agreement Number 31 to the Intergovernmental Agreement between
the  Bureau of Indian Affairs-Navajo Region and Navajo County that authorizes funding
assistance to provide gravel material on BIA Route 8065 within the Central Agency. 
Background:
The Black Mesa Chapter has been advocating for some years to maintain route 8065 for the
community members which have been facing adversity each year through the inclimate
weather. The chapter has been focused on fixing up drainage crossings and roadway surfaces to
make the road passable for students and elders. Due to the limited funding for the BIA-Chinle
Agency they have gone forth to request for gravel material to be applied on both sides of the
upcoming drainage crossing. This partnership with Navajo DOT and BIA will plan to gravel an
estimate of 1 mile of roadway. In which County will provide the material while Navajo DOT
and BIA will provide the workforce to prepare and apply gravel. 
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends Approval of Task Agreement No. 31
Fiscal Impact
Fiscal Year: 2023
Budgeted Y/N: Y
Amount Requested: 100,000
Fiscal Impact:
$100,000 will be allocated from District 1 Special Road Funds. 
Attachments
Task Agreement No. 31 
Request Letter 
Form Review

funding of purchasing Gravel Material at the request of Chinle BIA office, in support of 
road maintenance activities on BIA route 8065, which is located on the Navajo Indian 
Reservation within BIA Chinle Agency, in Navajo County.  
The BIA warrants and represents that the materials purchased with the Allocated Funds 
will be used primarily for maintenance activities on roads within Navajo County, which 
are open to the public and are eligible for the expenditure of HURF monies under the 
Arizona Constitution and related Arizona Revised Statutes. The BIA to fully cooperate in 
any audit of the Allocated Funds by the Arizona Auditor General. 
For any material acquired in whole or in part with the Allocated Funds, the acquiring 
party shall be solely responsible for all expenses, including without limitation preventive 
maintenance, repairs and insurance. The County shall have no responsibility or liability 
whatsoever in regard to such material. The BIA shall be responsible for identifying, 
prioritizing, scheduling and performing all maintenance activities for which the Allocated 
Funds are used in whole or in part. It is understood that the County’s sole obligation 
hereunder is to make payment of the Allocated Funds. 
Any Allocated Funds that are unused as of June 30, 2023 shall no longer be available 
for distribution pursuant to this Memorandum of Agreement. The Allocated Funds under 
this agreement shall be paid by Navajo County directly to the invoicing party upon the 
County’s receipt of invoices or billing statement that show that the items have been 
purchased and approved by the BIA. The total of all invoices shall not exceed the 
approved amount from this agreement.

RE: GRAVEL PLACEMENT ON N8065 
 
Dear Ms. Benally, 
 
The Chinle Agency Branch of Road Maintenance is requesting for Navajo County’s financial 
assistance for purchasing gravel and the placement of material on BIA Route N8065. The 
monetary assistance and labor to process  gravel material w ould be greatly apprec iated. The 
improvements to roadway would benefit  local travelers traveling during inclement weather 
conditions (snow and rain).  T he improvements have been a reoccurring topic brought to our 
attention, essential for the safety of the traveling community.  But with the limit funding the 
Chinle Agency – Branch of Road Maintenance receives annually.  It’s almost impossible for the 
program to purchase and placement without significantly hindering our annual road maintenance 
budget.  The local communities and our organizations within this area that utilize N8065 would 
greatly appreciate the assistance for this project.  
 
It’s our understanding that Navajo County can make this request possible with funding they 
have obtained.  Again, your assistance  will greatly be appreciated by the surrounding 
communities and other organizations that would greatly benefit from this effort. 
 
Your favorable review and approval are appreciated. 
 
If you have any questions don’t hesitate to contact Mr. Lorenzo Dugi, Chinle Agency - Branch 
of Road Maintenance at Office: 928-674-5176 to personally discuss the specific details of the 
plans and preparations for the event. 
 
 
      Sincerely, 
 
 
 
      Lorenzo Dugi 
      Agency Engineer, Chinle

Board of Supervisors Regular 6. d.        
Meeting Date: 05/23/2023  
Title: Task Agreement No. 32-N602
Submitted For: Madhav Mundle, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Miles Begay, Tribal Government Relations Liaison
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of Task Agreement Number 32 to the Intergovernmental Agreement between
the Bureau of Indian Affairs-Navajo Region and Navajo County that authorizes funding
assistance to provide asphalt material on BIA Route 602 within the Fort Defiance Agency. 
Background:
The N602 route has various locations that require pavement spot repairs due to pavement
distress and the freeze thaw action has made the conditions worse. Upon the request from the
Teesto Chapter, BIA has scheduled the pavement repair work for the month of June and has
requested Navajo County to supply asphaltic concrete material for pavement repairs. As a
partnering effort to facilitate the pavement repairs, Navajo County will provide the asphaltic
concrete material and BIA will provide the labor and equipment to complete the required
maintenance of the said roadways.
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval
Fiscal Impact
Fiscal Year: 2023
Budgeted Y/N: Y
Amount Requested: 60,000
Fiscal Impact:
$60,000 will be allocated from District 2 Navajo Nation HURF

: Assistance with Hot Asphalt Pavement material to repair Ruts on N60, N15 and N602

ear Mr. Peshlakai:

roadway pavement that had rutted and/or heaved causing the road to be unsafe to the traveling public. The Navajo 
County will assist with approximately 500 tons of new hot asphalt pavement and delivery to the repair site for the 
above-mentioned BIA routes. The eq uipment and manpower will be provided by the BI A Branch of Road 
Maintenance (BORM) along with tack coat material and water to be furnish by BIA BORM.

uring our previous discussions, we have found i t to be be neficial to Fort Defiance BOT and the Navajo County 
to have these BIA System Roads repaired as soon as possible to avoid any major accident or a tort claim.

Board of Supervisors Regular 6. e.        
Meeting Date: 05/23/2023  
Title: Task Agreement No. 33- N60/N15 Pavement
Submitted For: Madhav Mundle, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Miles Begay, Tribal Government Relations Liaison
Department: Public Works
Presented By: Madhav Mundle
Motion before the Board:
Consideration of Task Agreement Number 33 to the Intergovernmental Agreement between
the Bureau of Indian Affairs-Navajo Region and Navajo County that authorizes funding
assistance to provide asphalt material on BIA Route 60 & 15 within the Fort Defiance Agency. 
Background:
As the BIA coordinates work for N602, they plan to focus on some other pavement repairs in
the region. One of those sections will be on route 60 which has been a traveling hazard for over
a decade and with the expected volume of traffic to rise once the new medical center opens up
this will be a bigger issue if not repaired. Also additional pothole repairs along route 15 which
stretches across the county will be addressed through the coming weeks by Fort Defiance
Agency. This will be another partnership in which we supply the hot asphalt material for BIA
while they coordinate and perform the repairs throughout southwest Navajo. 
Recommendation:

Board of Supervisors Regular 6. f. 1.        
Meeting Date: 05/23/2023  
Title: Fee Schedule Planning, Zoning And Building Related Reviews And Permits
Submitted For: Mischa Larisch, Deputy Director of Planning & Development 
Submitted By: Mischa Larisch, Deputy Director of Planning & Development
Department: Public Works Division: Planning & Development
Presented By: Mischa Larisch
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 13 -
2023 approving/denying fee schedule for services provided to the public by the Planning and
Development Services Division of the Public Works Department in connection with Planning,
Zoning and Building Related Reviews and Permits.
Background:
Navajo County Board of Supervisors Adopted the International Building Code (IBC) 2018 and
International Residential Code (IRC) 2018 standards of construction in March of 2022.  Even
though new standards were adopted, valuation and fee structures remained as adopted in April
of 2010.  The Planning and Development Services Division of Public Works analyzed the fee
schedule and determined the County needed to update their building valuation data and fee
schedule.
The Planning and Development Services Division reviewed other jurisdictions fee schedules
and found most jurisdictions that adopt IBC and IRC standards for construction also adopt the
building valuation data and permit fee structures.  Below is from the International Code Council
IBC 2018.
The International Code Council is pleased to provide the following Building Valuation Data
(BVD) for its members. The BVD will be updated at six-month intervals. The BVD table
provides the “average” construction costs per square foot, which can be used in determining
permit fees for a jurisdiction. The permit fees can be established by using the BVD table and a
Permit Fee Multiplier, which is based on the total construction value within the jurisdiction for
the past year. The Square Foot Construction Cost table presents factors that reflect relative
value of one construction classification/occupancy group to another so that more expensive
construction is assessed greater permit fees than less expensive construction. ICC has developed
this data to aid jurisdictions in determining permit fees. It is important to note that while this
BVD table does determine an estimated value of a building (i.e., Gross Area x Square Foot
Construction Cost), this data is only intended to assist jurisdictions in determining their permit
fees. This data table is not intended to be used as an estimating guide because the data only
reflects average costs and is not representative of specific construction. This degree of precision
is enough for the intended purpose, which is to help establish permit fees to fund code

<!-- page 192 -->
compliance activities. This BVD table provides jurisdictions with a simplified way to determine
the estimated value of a building that does not rely on the permit applicant to determine the cost
of construction.
We called or looked up on their websites the following municipalities/Counties for reference on
their valuation and fees with building permits:
Show Low Adopted IBC 2018 with 2018 BVD and fee schedule
Snowflake Adopted IBC 2018 with 2018 BVD and fee schedule

but spreadsheet may have a multiplier reducing the valuation slightly from the BVD schedule.
Pinetop adopted 2018 IBC last year still using 2006 BVD but looking to change this budget
cycle to 2018 BVD and fee schedule
Springerville Adopted IBC 2015 with BVD and fee schedule in 2021
Eagar Adopted 2018 IBC using 2011 BVD but looking to adopt 2018 BVD and fee schedule
this budget cycle.

STAFF REPORT  
 
TO:    Board of Supervisors 
 
FROM:   Mischa Larisch, Deputy Director Planning and Development 
    Services 
  
HEARING DATE:  May 23, 2023 
 
PROJECT NUMBER: RESOLUTION ____-23 A RESOLUTION OF THE 
NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS, ADOPTING AND APPROVING 
FEE SCHEDULE FOR SERVICES PROVIDED TO THE PUBLIC BY THE 
PLANNING AND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES DIVISION OF THE PUBLIC 
WORKS DEPARTMENT IN CONNECTION WITH PLANNING, ZONING AND 
BUILDING RELATED REVIEWS AND PERMITS 
 
 
The Navajo County fee schedule for planning, zoning and building related services was last 
adopted April 27, 2010.   
 
BACKGROUND: 
 
Navajo County Board of Supervisors Adopted the International Building Code (IBC) 2018 and 
International Residential Code (IRC) 2018 standards of construction in March of 2022.  Even 
though new standards were adopted, valuation and fee structures remained as adopted in April 
of 2010.  The Planning and Development Services Division of Public Works analyzed the fee 
schedule and determined the County needed to update their  building valuation data and fee 
schedule. 
 
The Planning and Development Services Division reviewed other jurisdictions fee schedules 
and found most jurisdictions that adopt IBC and IRC standards for construction also adopt the 
building valuation data and permit fee structures.  Below is from the International Code 
Council IBC 2018. 
 
The International Code Council is pleased to provide the  following Building Valuation Data 
(BVD) for its members. The  BVD will be updated at six -month intervals . The BVD table

<!-- page 194 -->
2 | P a g e  
 
 
provides the “average” construction costs per  square foot, which can be used in determining  
permit fees for a jurisdiction. The permit fees can be established by using the BVD table and a 
Permit Fee Multiplier, which is based on the total construction value within the jurisdiction for 
the past year. The Square Foot  Construction Cost table presents factors  that reflect relative 
value of one construction classification/occupancy group to  another so that more expensive 
construction is assessed  greater permit fees than less expensive  construction. ICC has 
developed this data to aid jurisdictions in determining  permit fees. It is important to note that 
while this BVD table does determine an estimated value of a building (i.e., Gross Area x Square 
Foot Construction Cost), this data is only  intended to assist jurisdictions in determining their 
permit fees. This data table is not intended to be used as an estimating guide because the data 
only reflects average costs  and is not  representative of specific construction.  This degree of 
precision is enough for the intended purpose,  which is to help establish permit fees to fund 
code compliance activities. This BVD table provides jurisdictions  with a simplified way to  
determine the estimated value of a  building that does not rely on the permit applicant to  
determine the cost of construction. 
 
We called or looked up on their websites the following municipalities /Counties for reference 
on their valuation and fees with building permits: 
 
Show Low Adopted IBC 2018 with 2018 BVD and fee schedule 
Snowflake Adopted IBC 2018 with 2018 BVD and fee schedule 
Taylor have an excel spreadsheet. I understand they are using 2015 IBC fee schedule and BVD 
but spreadsheet may have a multiplier reducing the valuation slightly from the BVD schedule. 
Pinetop adopted 2018 IBC last year still using 2006 BVD but looking to change this budget 
cycle to 2018 BVD and fee schedule 
Springerville Adopted IBC 2015 with BVD and fee schedule in 2021 
Eagar Adopted 2018 IBC using 2011 BVD but looking to adopt 2018 BVD  and fee schedule 
this budget cycle. 
Apache County Adopted IBC 2015 with 2015 BVD and fee schedule 
Gila County Adopted IBC 2012 with 2012 BVD and fee schedule in 2014 
 
STAFF ANALYSIS: 
 
While the main area needing to be addressed is valuation, the 1997 permit fee schedule was 
also slightly out of date.  In doing the review for building related services, planning and zoning 
fees were also reviewed with certain services requiring adjustment to better reflect the costs 
associated with those services.

RESOLUTION ____-23 
 
A RESOLUTION OF THE NAVAJO COUNTY BOARD OF SUPERVISORS, 
ADOPTING AND APPROVING FEE SCHEDULE FOR SERVICES PROVIDED TO 
THE PUBLIC BY THE PLANNING AND DEVELOPMENT SERVICES DIVISION OF 
THE PUBLIC WORKS DEPARTMENT IN CONNECTION WITH PLANNING, 
ZONING AND BUILDING RELATED REVIEWS AND PERMITS 
 
WHEREAS, the Board of Supervisors is authorized by A.R.S § 11.251.08 to adopt fee 
schedules for specific products and services that Navajo County provides to the public, and 
 
WHEREAS, the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance and Building Safety Code provide that fees 
for planning, zoning and building related reviews and permits shall be in accordance with fee 
schedules adopted and amended by the Board of Supervisors from time to time, and  
 
WHEREAS, the Board of Supervisors approved Resolution 9-2022 adopting the International 
Building Code (IBC) 2018 and the International Residential Code (IRC) 2018 March 22, 2022, 
and  
 
WHEREAS, the Development Services Division of Public Works has recommended to the 
Board of Supervisors the adoption of a new fee schedule for planning, zoning and building 
related services fees set forth in Exhibit 1 hereto, and 
 
WHEREAS, following a duly noticed public hearing held this date the Board finds the 
recommended fee schedule does not exceed the actual costs of providing planning, zoning and 
building related services and that the fee schedule is in proper form and should be adopted 
pursuant to A.R.S § 11.251.08, 
 
NOW THEREFORE BE IT RESOLVED by the Board of Supervisors that the fee schedule 
attached hereto as Exhibit 1 is hereby adopted for the public services described herein.

<!-- page 201 -->
Important Points 
 
• The BVD is not intended to apply to alterations or 
repairs to existing buildings. Because the scope of 
alterations or repairs to an existing building varies so 
greatly, the Square Foot Construction Costs table 
does not reflect accurate values for that purpose. 
However, the Square Foot Construction Costs table 
can be used to determine the cost of an addition that is 
basically a stand- alone building which happens to be 
attached to an existing building. In the case of such 
additions, the only alterations to the existing building 
would involve the attachment of the addition to the 
existing building and the openings between the 
addition and the existing building. 
 
• For purposes of establishing the Permit Fee Multiplier, 
the estimated total annual construction value for a 
given time period (1 year) is the sum of each building’s 
value (Gross Area x Square Foot Construction Cost) 
for that time period (e.g., 1 year). 
 
• The Square Foot Construction Cost does not include 
the price of the land on which the building is built. The 
Square Foot Construction Cost takes into account 
everything from foundation work to the roof structure 
and coverings but does not include the price of the 
land. The cost of the land does not affect the cost of 
related code enforcement activities and is not included 
in the Square Foot Construction Cost.

Navajo County Board of Supervisors Adopted the International Building Code (IBC) 2018 and International Residential Code 
(IRC) 2018 standards of construction in March of 2022.  Even though new standards were adopted, valuation and fee 
structures remained as adopted in April of 2010.  The Planning and Development Division of Public Works analyzed the fee 
schedule and determined the County needed to update their building valuation data and fee schedule. The 2010 adopted fee 
schedule uses a set cost per square foot for building valuation (residential is set at $70 per square foot) and 1997 IBC fee 
schedule for permit cost. 
The Planning and Development Services Division reviewed other jurisdictions fee schedules and found most jurisdictions that 
adopt IBC and IRC standards for construction also adopt the building valuation data (BVD) and permit fee structures.

building permits:

Title: COMP 23-001 Update to Navajo County Comprehensive Plan
Submitted By: Cody Cooper, Planning Manager
Department: Public Works Division: Planning & Development
Presented By: Cody Cooper
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and possible adoption of Resolution Number 14 -
2023 approving/denying an update to the Comprehensive Plan of Navajo County.
Background:
The Navajo County Comprehensive Plan was last adopted by the Navajo County Board of
Supervisors on May 24, 2011.  Planning and Development Services have designed the update
of the Comprehensive Plan to meet all relevant requirements of A.R.S. § 11-804.
Navajo County Planning and Development Services Staff are recommending an update of the
Comprehensive Plan.  This update contains the following changes to the Comprehensive Plan:
  
Addition of a hearing process for major amendments.  This hearing process would allow
requests for major amendments to be received from January 1st until June 31st of each
calendar year.  One hearing for these major amendments would then be held in October
of every year, provided there are applications received by Staff.  This process would
require the affirmative vote of four of the five Supervisors to adopt major amendments
and is designed to be in compliance with A.R.S. § 11-805.
Updating of figures to reflect changing statistics within Navajo County.
Rewriting of sections to allow for greater readability of the document for the public.
Updating of goals and policies to be easier to read and understand.
Breaking apart goals and policies to make them more defined and cohesive.
Removal of goals and policies which have been achieved since the adoption of the 2011
Comprehensive Plan.
Updates to referenced statutes, to ensure correctness.
Stylistic changes to allow for greater readability.
At the Thursday, April 20, 2023 Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission Hearing, the
Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission unanimously recommended approval of this
update to the Navajo County Comprehensive Plan.

           STAFF REPORT  
 
TO:  Board of Supervisors 
 
FROM:  Cody Cooper, Planning Manager 
  
HEARING DATE:  May 23, 2023  
 
PROJECT NUMBER:  COMP 23 -001 UPDATE TO NAVAJO COUNTY 
COMPREHENSIVE PLAN:  Consideration of an update to the 
Comprehensive Plan of Navajo County. 
 
 
 
 
BACKGROUND 
 
The Navajo County Comprehensive Plan was last adopted by the Navajo County Board of 
Supervisors on May 24, 2011.  Planning and Development Services have designed the update of the 
Comprehensive Plan to meet all relevant requirements of A.R.S. § 11-804. 
 
Navajo County Planning and Development Services Staff are recommending an update of the 
Comprehensive Plan.  This update contains the following changes to the Comprehensive Plan: 
 
- Addition of a hearing process for major amendments.  This hearing process would allow requests 
for major amendments to be received from January 1st until June 31st of each calendar year.  One 
hearing for these major amendments would then be held in October of every year, provided there 
are applications received by Staff.  This process would require the affirmative vote of four of the 
five Supervisors to adopt major amendments and is designed to be in compliance with A.R.S. § 
11-805. 
- Updating of figures to reflect changing statistics within Navajo County. 
- Rewriting of sections to allow for greater readability of the document for the public. 
- Updating of goals and policies to be easier to read and understand. 
- Breaking apart goals and policies to make them more defined and cohesive. 
- Removal of goals and policies which have been achieved since the adoption of the 2011 
Comprehensive Plan. 
- Updates to referenced statutes, to ensure correctness. 
- Stylistic changes to allow for greater readability.

STAFF ANALYSIS 
 
Navajo County Planning and Development Staff ha ve been working on the proposed update to the 
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan in order to ensure compliance with all State laws related to this 
document. 
 
Staff sent out the proposed Comprehensive Plan update to all required jurisdictions on Thursday, 
February 9, 2023 to allow for at least a sixty (60) day comment period prior to any hearings related 
to adopting this update.  Three responses were received in this comment period.  These responses 
came from Coconino County, Pinetop-Lakeside, and Snowflake.  All of the comments received were 
limited to simple clarifying questions.   
 
During the sixty (60) day commenting period Staff provided information regarding the proposed 
update, with directions for comments on the Planning and Development Services webpage.  This 
information was included both on the main page and the Comprehensive Plan page.  No comments 
were received from the public via the website. 
 
Planning and Development Staff also hosted public meetings to educate the public, answer 
questions, and take comments on the proposed Comprehensive Plan Update.  These meetings were 
held at the following times and locations: 
 
Tuesday, March 28, 2023 
6:00 p.m. – 7:00 p.m. 
Board of Supervisors Chambers 
100 E. Code Talkers Dr 
Holbrook, AZ 86025 
 
Thursday, March 30, 2023 
6:00 – 7:00 p.m. 
Timber Mesa Admin Building 
3561 E. Deuce of Clubs 
Show Low, AZ 85901 
 
Staff did not receive any comments from the public during the hosted public meetings. 
 
Within the attachments for this item, the Commission will find the proposed Comprehensive Plan 
Update, the currently adopted Comprehensive Plan, and comments from neighboring jurisdictions. 
 
At the Thursday, April 20, 2023 Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commission Hearing, the 
Navajo County Planning and Zoning Commissi on unanimously recommended approval of this 
update to the Navajo County Comprehensive Plan.

Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  1 | P a g e  
 
1.0 Summary  
1.1 Overview  
Navajo County is required by State law to have a Comprehensive Plan. The purpose of the 
Comprehensive Plan is to guide and accomplish coordinated, adjusted, and harmonious development 
of Navajo County, pursuant to the present and future needs of the County. Navajo County’s 
Comprehensive Plan has been developed to conserve the County’s natural resources, ensure efficient 
expenditure of public monies and promote the health, safety, convenience and general welfare of the 
public. 
This document is the latest version of Navajo County’s Comprehensive Plan  (“The Plan”) . The 
Comprehensive Plan is a living document that can be amended at any time through proper procedures. 
The Comprehensive Plan must be updated at least every ten years. The Plan assists the Board of 
Supervisors, Planning and Zoning Commission, and County Staff in making land-use decisions.  
The Comprehensive Plan is a document that can help the County grow and preserve its customs, 
cultural, and rural historical values in a logical and orderly way and respond to future challenges. This 
Plan is a vision of the future in which each resident of the County has a stake and the County welcomes 
and encourages the participation of all residents as The Plan evolves over the years. 
The planning process is structured to emphasize public involvement and incorporate comments, ideas, 
and direction of the public into The Plan. This plan strives to create strong and vibrant communities 
within Navajo County by encouraging orderly development.  
The United States Census lists the 2020 population for Navajo County as 106,717 persons as of April 
1, 2020. The Arizona Office of Economic Opportunity estimates that the population of Navajo County 
will decrease to nearly 99,574 by 2050. It can be challenging to assess the total population within 
Navajo County due to a large amount of second/vacation homes and a significant “phantom” 
population, which are groups of people not reflected in census numbers or other statistics.  Due to this, 
careful preparation is required to accommodate the total population of Navajo County and maintain 
the character and quality of life that residents of the County find so important.  
1.2 Definitions Used in This Plan 
Area Plan: A designation given to a specific land area on the map that indicates how t hat area may 
develop over time using specific land use designations, guidelines, goals and/or objectives. An Area 
Plan shall encompass at least 640 acres.  
Character Area: A designation given to a general land area on the map that indicates how that area 
may develop over time using general guidelines, possible density limitations, etc.  
Development: As used in this plan,  those areas where the property owner(s) desire to initiate a 
subdivision plat, a zone change in order to process a subdivision plat, or initiate site plans for possible 
new commercial/industrial locations, etc., according to State statute and Navajo County Ordinances.  
Growth: Experiencing more parcel splits per State statutes, platting of subdivisions, or an increase of 
in-fill of vacant parcels, etc.  
Special Development : Land under unified control to be developed in a single development or a 
programmed series of phases that incorporate mixed uses of housing types, commercial operations,

<!-- page 236 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  2 | P a g e  
 
etc. A "Special Development" includes the provisions, operations, maintenance, facilities , and 
improvements that will be for the common use of the development district, but which is not normally 
to be maintained at general public expense.  
Subdivision: Improved or unimproved land or lands divided or proposed to be divided for the purpose 
of sale or lease, whether immediate or in the future, according to State statutes.  
Zoning Ordinance : The ordinance adopted by Navajo County that regulates and controls the 
character and use of property to protect the public safety, health, and welfare.  
1.3 How to Use The Plan  
The Comprehensive Plan provides a guide for decisions by the Planning and Zoning Commission and 
the Board of Supervisors concerning land use growth and development, which include s serving as a 
basis for decisions regarding updates to the zoning map and approval of subdivision plats. It provides 
an understanding of existing and proposed land use for the public, decision-makers, and developers.  
This Plan is advisory in nature; it is not a regulatory document.  The Plan establishes a long-range 
vision for development of Navajo County and is a foundation for change. Its goals and policies aim 
for ideal scenarios, meaning that goals can occasionally be in conflict with one another. Examples of 
this could be issues related to the geography of sites or circumstances specific to certain proposals. As 
such, the Comprehensive Plan guides the decision-making process. 
A primary concern for the County must be fair resolution of conflicting interests.  Political, social, 
economic, and educational institutions must work together to address their varied interests, values, 
desires, and perception of what goals, objectives, and strategies are needed for growth and 
development in the County. Public involvement is essential in the development of these broadly stated 
goals. 
The abovementioned goals are the foundation upon which land use policies and proposals have been 
constructed to help address specific and widely differing individual interests and integrate them on a 
Countywide basis through the vehicle of the Comprehensive Plan. 
Revisions to the text of the County Zoning Ordinance (“Zoning Ordinance”)  may be necessary in 
order to achieve the goals, policies, and concepts of this Plan. Subdivision Regulations and 
Requirements may also be amended for the same reason. The Plan is based upon the premise that the 
County may review the Zoning Ordinance to see if adjustments are necessary, as requested, or where 
a demonstrated need is shown. This includes adding to or revising zoning district definitions, adding 
new designations, etc.   
Character areas in the Land Use Element, discussed below, suggest developm ent standards that the 
County could include in its revision of the Subdivision Regulations and Requirements to mitigate 
impacts between land uses, provide protection of existing land uses, give land owners support as to 
what development is possible upon th eir land and adjoining properties, and continue to provide 
flexibility in potential uses of property. 
1.4 Land Use  
Given the vast area and primarily rural character of Navajo County, character areas in The Plan 
represent generalized land use, development,  or preservation concepts that recognize and promote 
existing development patterns.  The character areas take the place of more traditional land use

<!-- page 237 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  3 | P a g e  
 
categories, as this Comprehensive Plan is intended to set a general framework for development in the 
County rather than establish a more precise guide that mirrors a zoning district map.  
Upon approval by the Board of Supervisors of The Plan, the County will use the Comprehensive Plan 
to develop Zoning Ordinance and/or Subdivision Regulations & Requirements revi sions for The 
Board of Supervisors ’ consideration. As noted in Section 1.2, such revisions are necessary to 
accomplish the goals, policies and concepts contained in this Plan. The designated Area Plans indicate 
the proposed land uses within their respective areas.  
1.5 Circulation  
The circulation system will provide for the efficient and safe movement of persons and goods within 
and through Navajo County. This system is to be environmentally compatible with the surrounding 
conditions and supportive of economic development. Most of the development within the County has 
occurred or will occur, along major, paved transportation corridors. As such, the circulation system is 
not only responsible for circulation but also directing areas of growth within the County,  with the 
intersections of well -traveled transportation routes being the locations of greatest growth potential. 
Priority should also be given to school bus routes.  
1.6 Applicability  
This Comprehensive Plan applies only to unincorporated areas of Navajo County . The Plan does 
not apply to the incorporated areas  or land that is currently part of the Hopi, Navajo, or Whi te 
Mountain Apache Reservations or within the boundaries of a reservation. When the respective tribal 
nations develop plans that approach their borders of the non -reservation areas of the County, these 
plans should be linked and/or integrated at those boun daries into the Comprehensive Plan, as 
appropriate. Given these exceptions, The Plan focuses primarily on the central to southern portion of 
the County, with the majority of the covered area lying south of Interstate 40.  
The following area or community plans in the unincorporated areas of Navajo County are referenced 
herein and support the Comprehensive Plan. The Comprehensive Plan Land Use Map, discussed in 
Section 4.4, references the Area Plans of:  
Heber-Overgaard  
Pinetop-Lakeside/Navajo County Regional Plan 
Aztec Area Plan  
1.7 Previous Comprehensive Plans 
This Comprehensive Plan shall replace all previous Comprehensive Plans and is an Amendment and 
update to the Comprehensive Plan adopted  on May 24, 2011 . Upon approval by the Board of 
Supervisors, this amendment and update shall serve to replace in its entirety the Comprehensive Plan 
adopted on May 24, 2011.  
1.8 Amendments to the Comprehensive Plan 
An amendment to the Comprehensive Plan shall be necessary when a proposed land use for a property 
does not conform to land uses established in this Plan for the property’s designated character area, 
discussed in the Land Use Element . The character areas included in this Plan allow a wide and

<!-- page 238 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  4 | P a g e  
 
extensive range of land uses. Virtually every existing zoning designation is allowed in every character 
area.  
Given the breadth of permissible uses in the character areas, few changes in land use will require a 
Comprehensive Plan amendment.  All requests to amend the Navajo County Comprehensive Plan 
shall be considered minor amendments unless they meet the criteria for a major amendment, as listed 
below.  Both scrivener’s errors and typographical errors are considered neither major , nor minor, 
amendments and can be corrected administratively. 
A major amendment is one or more of the following:  
▪ A substantial alteration of the County’s land use mixture or balance as established in the 
Comprehensive Plan Land Use Element for the area of the County to which the Comprehensive 
Plan applies.  
 
▪ A change from the Recreation, Highway Service, or Environmentally Sensitive Development 
Area character areas to any other character area that encompasses more than 500 acres.  
 
▪ A change to any of the other character areas that encompasses more than 1,000 acres.  
 
▪ Adding a new Area Plan or Rural Planning area, or a change to an Area Plan or Rural Planning 
Area that meets any of the above criteria. 
  
▪ A change in text which alters any of the goals or policies within The Plan. 
 
▪ Adding a new element or substanti ally changing an existing element of The Plan prior to the 
required ten (10) year update. 
 
Major amendment requests shall be accepted by the Planning and Development Department from 
January 1st of a calendar year until June 31st of the same calendar year.  All major amendments shall 
be heard once per calendar year and shall be considered by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors 
at their first scheduled meeting in October.  Major amendments require affirmative votes of four (4) 
of the five (5) County Supervisors in order to be adopted.   Major amendments are subject to 
referendum, as outlined in Article IV, Part 1, Secti on 1, Subsection 8 of the Constitution of Arizona 
and Title 19, Chapter 1, Article 4 of the Arizona Revised Statutes.  The public hearing process for a 
Major Amendment must satisfy the requirements of A.R.S. § 11-805J.

<!-- page 239 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  5 | P a g e  
 
2.0 Introduction  
2.1 Role and Purpose of the Comprehensive Plan  
County government is required by State law to prepare a Comprehensive Plan for the unincorporated 
areas (A.R.S. §11-804). This Plan shall address challenges facing the County and present opportunities 
for the continuing success and diversity of the region by:  
▪ Encouraging coordinated physical development in accordance with the present and future needs 
of the County.  
 
▪ Promoting stewardship of, and respect for, the natural and cultural resources of the County. 
  
▪ Guiding growth to achieve efficient expenditure of public funds.  
 
▪ Protecting investments in private property. 
 
▪ Promoting the health, safety, convenience, and general welfare of the public.  
 
▪ Encouraging economic growth and diversity.  
 
The planning process is structured to emphasize public involvement and incorporate comments, ideas, 
and direction of the public into The Plan.  
The statute requires counties to prepare a Comprehensive Plan to guide coordinated, adjusted, and 
harmonious development within their jurisdictions. Navajo County has elected to include a Land Use 
Element and a Circulation Element. Navajo County incorporates planning for growth areas in its 
Land Use Element.  
2.2 Land Use Element  
The Land Use Element includes the following:  
▪ Planning for land use that describes the proposed character areas of uses for land appropriate to 
Navajo County. The character areas refle ct Navajo County’s vision for the area and discuss the 
types of development, such as residential, commercial, and recreational, which are expected to 
help the County and its residents realize this vision. This planning includes:  
 
o Consideration of air quality and access to incident solar and wind energy for all character 
areas.  
 
o Strategies that address maintaining a broad variety of land uses including the range of uses 
existing in the County at the time the plan is adopted, readopted, or amended.  
 
▪ Planning for growth areas, specifically identifying those areas that are particularly suitable for 
infrastructure expansion and improvements designed to support a concentration of land uses, such 
as residential, commercial, and tourism uses. This planning includes:

<!-- page 240 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  6 | P a g e  
 
o A possible method to make infrastructure expansion more economical and to provide for 
a rational pattern of land development.  
 
o Goals to conserve significant natural resources and open areas in growth areas and to 
coordinate their location with similar areas outside of the growth areas.  
 
o Possible promotion of public and private construction of timely and financially sound 
infrastructure expansion through the use of infrastructure funding and financing planning 
that is coordinated with development activity. 
  
▪ Addressing the cost of development through identification of policies and strategies that 
Navajo County could use to require development to pay its fair share toward the cost of  
additional public facility needs generated from new development. The information includes: 
  
o A component discussing the mechanisms allowed by law to fund and finance 
additional public services necessary to serve a new development, including special 
taxing districts, development fees, in lieu fees and facility construction, dedications, 
and privatization.  
 
o A component discussing policies that will ensure that any financing/funding 
mechanism will result in a beneficial use to the new development, bear a reasonable 
relationship to the burden imposed on Navajo County to provide additional necessary 
public facil ities to the new development, and be imposed in conformance to legal 
requirements.  
2.3 Circulation Element  
The Circulation Element includes the following: 
▪ Planning for circulation consisting of the general location and extent of existing and proposed 
interstate highways, State highways, paved County highways, non -paved but maintained County 
roadways, primitive roads, and other modes of transportation.  
▪ Correlation of circulation planning with the Land Use Element to direct growth in Navajo County.  
2.4 Planning Process  
Planning is a process wherein the vision of what the County may look like in the future is put down 
on paper. Through public input, participation in meetings, etc., this vision is created as a guide to all. 
In January of 2000, The Navajo County Board of Supervisors adopted a public participation 
procedures ordinance as part of the growing smarter legislation. This directs Staff to involve the public 
as much as possible in order to obtain their input in such matters. The procedures require: 
• Transmit the Draft Plan to the Board of Supervisors, each municipality in the County, all 
counties contiguous to the County, the Northern Arizona Council o f Governments, the 
Arizona Department of Commerce – or any other state agency that is designated as the general 
planning agency for Arizona, and any person or entity that requests a copy in writing. This 
transmittal is to be completed at least 60 days before adoption of the Plan.

<!-- page 241 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  7 | P a g e  
 
• A 60-day review period during which any interested party may submit written comments on 
the Plan to the County. During this period, the County must conduct at least one public 
workshop regarding the Plan to which interested parties and the public will be invited. The 
invitations are to be extended to persons representing all geographic, ethnic, and economic 
groups in the County.  
 
• Full public hearings before the Planning and Zoning Commission and the Board of 
Supervisors.

<!-- page 242 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  8 | P a g e  
 
3.0 Background Information 
3.1 History 
In 1864, the first Arizona legislature set up four Counties, including Yavapai County. In 1879, Apache 
County was created out of the eastern two fifths of Yavapai County. On March 21, 1895, Apache 
County was halved, and the western portion became Navajo County, as the final act of the Territorial 
Assembly. By the time the land was officially Navajo County, the area had already begun to develop. 
The railroad had crossed the County for more than a decade and North America’s third largest ranch, 
the Aztec Land and Cattle Company , near Holbrook, had been established. Holbrook, the County 
seat, was founded in 1881. 
3.2 Population 
Navajo County is home to several incorporated towns and cities and numerous unincorporated  
communities. The information below details the populations in the incorporated areas according to 
the United States Census Bureau, with projected population changes from the Arizona Office of 
Economic Opportunity (OEO). 
The County population increased steadily from 1970 until 2010 but  is now projected to decrease to 
just under 100,000 persons by 2050.   While population increased through 2010, projections are 
expecting a steady decrease in population over the next 40 years.  Population numbers and projections 
for Navajo County are shown below.  
NAVAJO COUNTY POPULATION BY DECADE

 2010 107,521

 2050 99,574  
            2060   95,080 
The United States Census shows a population in Navajo County’s incorporated cities and towns as 
follows. The projection for 2050 is from the Arizona Office of Economic Opportunity (OEO). 
3.3 Physical Setting  
The Mogollon Rim divides the County into two distinct regions. The high country in the northern 
part of the County is arid and desert -like with empty flat-topped mesas, isolated buttes, valleys, and 
smaller plateaus. The primary vegetation is sagebrush, short grasses, and some juniper and piñon. The 
Little Colorado River is the key geographic feature of Navajo County. It flows generally west -
northwest, from the Apache County border on the east to the Navajo Indian Reservation boundary 
across the northern portion of the County to the Coconino County border on the west.

<!-- page 243 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  9 | P a g e  
 
The southern portion of the County is rugged mountain area, heavily wooded with piñon-juniper and 
ponderosa pine, with many lakes and streams. Elevations in Navajo County vary from 4,850 feet near 
Winslow to 7,575 feet at the Mogollon Rim.  
3.4 Land Ownership/Governance  
The vast majority of Navajo County is not in private ownership. Almost 66 percent (66%) of Navajo 
County’s 9,949 square miles is Native American Reservation Land. The US Forest Service (“USFS”) 
and Bureau of Land Management (“BLM”) together control 9% of t he County; the State of Arizona 
owns 5.9%. Individual and corporate ownership accounts for only 18% of the County. See Exhibits 
1A and 1B.  
In areas outside the Reservations and the National Forest, land is held in a checkerboard pattern of 
sections of lan d. A significant portion of the checkerboard involves State holdings and all of the 
following: Tribal Land in fee ownership, private land, BLM holdings, and subdivided land, as 
illustrated on Exhibits 1A and 1B, Existing Conditions. Unlike other Arizona co unties, State 
ownership includes no large blocks of contiguous sections. Two State properties are parks – Homolovi 
State Park outside Winslow and Fool Hollow Lake Recreation Area near Show Low.  Some private 
inholdings exist within the National Forest and within the Reservations.  
The Native American land includes several Reservations – the Navajo, Hopi, and White Mountain 
Apache. The northernmost incorporated community in Navajo County is Kayenta, which is part  of 
the Navajo Indian Reservation and was founded in 1909 as a trading post; Kayenta now serves as the 
gateway to Navajo Tribal Park at Monument Valley and a thriving Navajo community.  
Farther south is the Hopi Indian Reservation that is completely surrou nded by the Navajo 
Reservation. The Hopi Pueblo of Old Oraibi is one of the oldest continuously inhabited settlements 
in the United States. The White Mountain Apache Indian Reservation is located south of both Show 
Low and Pinetop/Lakeside and extends south to the Gila County line and east to the Greenlee County 
line.  
Navajo County contains the following incorporated communities: Holbrook  (the County seat ), 
Winslow, Snowflake, Taylor, Show Low, and Pinetop -Lakeside. Several other non -Reservation 
communities are unincorporated, including Adamana, Cedar Hills, Clay Springs, Heber, Joseph City, 
Linden, Overgaard, Pinedale, Shumway, Sun Valley, White Mountain Lake, and Woodruff. As noted 
in Section 1.5, Heber-Overgaard has an Area Plan. The Pinetop-Lakeside/Navajo County Regional 
Plan also covers some unincorporated territory that is not included in this Plan.  
3.5 Economy/Industry  
Navajo County’s principal industries are tourism, coal mining, manufacturing, timber production, and 
ranching. Other employers include all levels of government, Arizona Public Service’s Cholla Power 
Plant southwest of Joseph City, and the Burlington Northern Santa Fe Railway Company. Economic 
expansion will be a critical component of growth in the Co unty and will also provide better 
opportunities for the County’s youth to remain in, or return to, the area after finishing their schooling.  
3.6 Climate  
The climate of Navajo County is classified as semi -arid or sub -humid. This dry climate is a 
consequence of the low relative humidity and abundant sunshine that are prevalent for much of the 
year. Relative humidity may fall as low as 10% in June, when the sun shines 80% to 85% of daylight

<!-- page 244 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  10 | P a g e  
 
hours. Two or three days of total cloud cover is rare at any time of  the year, and five to eight 
consecutive days without a trace of clouds is a common occurrence during the dry months of May 
and June.  
Typically, 37% to 48% of the total annual precipitation falls within the three-month period from July 
to September. Often this occurs in the form of torrential thunderstorms, known as monsoons. 
Along the Little Colorado valley, in the lower elevations, winter snowfall may in rare instances impede 
traffic movement. In the higher elevations, snowfall often presents a danger both to the movement of 
traffic and to the unwary outdoor enthusiast.  
Wind velocities of 35 to 50 miles per hour are commonly observed in Winslow (situated in the 
northern area of the County where relatively flat valleys and mesas have little dampening effect on the 
wind) during March and April, the windiest months. Even higher velocities have been measured; 
however, the duration periods are short.

3.7 Soils  
There are nine basic soil conditions, and associations, in Navajo County. Each of these soil conditions 
is characteristically found in association with a general set of climatic, topographic and related 
physical features. As soil conditions and associations are obviously a major influence affecting 
development potentials, it is important to understand them.  
3.7.1 Deep Loamy and Sandy Soils  
These soils are mainly members of the Moffat and Sheppard series. Moffat soils are loamy or 
sandy on the surface, with sandy clay loam subsoil that has a distinct lime layer in the lower 
subsoil. These soils generally develop on gently rolling topography. Sheppard soils are deep , 
sandy, and occur on the broad, high ridges of old dunes. Both soil types are found at elevations

<!-- page 245 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  11 | P a g e  
 
of 5,000 to 5,700 feet, atop the long narrow mesas that slope sharply down to the eroded shale 
of the Painted Desert.  
Vegetation is short grass, typically, with sparse woody plants and weeds. Forage production 
is fairly high, although permeability is rapid. The total effect of this s oil association limits 
profitable development.  
3.7.2 Shale Badlands  
This area consists of dissected sandy, silty , and clay-like shale which occurs below the deep 
sandy loamy mesas of Association #1 . About 85% of the area is devoid of vegetation and 
classifies as badlands. Because of the polychromatic nature of the eroding Chinle shale, this 
area is known as the Painted Desert.  
The rest of the Association #2 area consists of deep alluvial soils of the Navajo, Jocity , and 
Ives series. These occur on narrow floodplains and fans, forming a shallow soil cover over the 
shale where surface-water and wind erosion is mitigated by the accumulation of log gravel and 
other stabilizing matter. All soils in this association are affected to some degree by the salts 
and alkali of the parent (underlying) shale.  
Elevation ranges from 4,500 to 5,500 feet and vegetation, when it manages to grow, is short 
grass. Development possibilities on the 85% area devoid of soil and vegetation are extremely 
limited and quite limited on the deeper alluvial soil-covered areas.  
3.7.3 The Flood Plains  
Alluvial soils on the floodplains of the Little Colorado River are members of the Jocity, Tours, 
Navajo, and Ives series. Textures range from clay to loamy fine sand and salinity varies from 
slight to severe, with slight to moderate alkali. A permanent water table is 6 feet or more below 
the floodplain’s level to gently sloping surface (accumulation of windblown material around 
clumps of grass and shrubs makes the plain appear hummocky in many places).  
Vegetation is browse type. Permeability is very slow and almost all cultivation in the County 
occurs on these soils. Potentials for other developments are limited, however, due to the 
hazards of periodic flooding.  
3.7.4 Shallow Soils on Sandstone and Sandy Shale  
These soils consist of the shallow, very shallow, and stony phases of the Moenkopi series and 
are found on interbedded sandstone and shale of the Moenkopi formation. Scattered through 
the western and central sections of this soil -association area are outcroppings and low ridges 
of dense sandstone; here the soil is very shallow.  
In t he eastern sections, shallow soil overlays sand and clay shale. Elevations in this soil -
association area are between 5,000 and 5,500 feet, with short-grass vegetation growing on the 
gently rolling topography. Permeability is rapid to very rapid. Depending upon soil depth, 
cultivation and development are limited.  
3.7.5 Brown Sandy Soils on Sandstone  
These soils are generally shallow and often stony. The surface is loam or fine sandy loam and 
the subsoil is loam and light clay loam. Usually, soils on sandstone are calcareous throughout

<!-- page 246 -->
Navajo County Comprehensive Plan – 2023 Update  12 | P a g e  
 
the profile and this is generally true of Association #5 soils. Certain small parcels, where the 
soil is on old outwash materials, have non-calcareous clay loam or clay subsoil.  
About 20% of this association area consists of sandstone outcroppings; these may be partially 
covered by thin layers of sand or loamy sand. The elevation range is 5,500 to 6,000 feet. 
Development potentials appear to be limited.  
3.7.6 Hilly, Gravelly, Shallow Soils  
These soils are found in association with small, rounded hills and sharp breaks over a parent 
material which is a mixture of old gravelly outwash , deposited on shallow sandstone and on 
silty or clay-like shale. Thickness of the gravelly outwash material now runs from a few inches 
to several feet. Texture of the surface soil ranges through loam, fine sandy loam and sandy 
loam to gravelly loam and gravelly sandy loam.  
The topography, at 5,500 to 6,000 feet, is gently to moderate rolling with 2% to 8% slopes. 
Vegetation is short-grass and soil permeability is slow. Development of the gently sloping areas 
is possible.  
3.7.7 Shale and Sandstone Rock Land  
Small buttes, ledges and knolls with active erosion distinguish this association area. More than 
70% of the land is barren shale and sandstone  rock. The rest consists of very shallow soils of 
the Moenkopi series, supporting sparse vegetation of the piñon-juniper type. This area has very 
little development potential.  
3.7.8 Soil on Basalt and Cinders  
In southeastern Navajo County is a 6,000 to 7,500-foot high plateau that is completely covered 
with basalt flows and cinder cones. Soils are very shallow to deep and textures range from clay 
to loam with the Springerville and Paiso series dominant. Some ou tcroppings of basalt are 
found. Vegetation reflects the differences in soil textures and varies from short grass to forest. 
Development is possible and cultivation of the finer-textured soils can be fruitful.  
3.7.9 Shallow to Deep Soils on the Mogollon Plateau  
On the Mogollon Plateau, soil associations differ greatly because of the wide variations in 
parent material, which progresses from sandstone to shale or limestone to sand and gravel. 
Some of the plateau’s soils belong to the Show Low, Millard, Elledge, Chevelon, Zeniff, and 
Overgaard series.  
Alluvial soils are of the Heber, Mogollon and Jacques series. Also evident are shallow to 
moderately deep soils of sand or clay loam over limestone, sandstone , and shale. Elevations 
range from 6,000 t o 7,000 feet. Vegetation is forest (primarily Ponderosa Pine) and piñon -
juniper.

<!-- page 247 -->
